I guess… the main thing is that I keep making myself throw up after I eat. It’s hard to say out loud, honestly. It’s gotten to where it feels like the only thing that calms me down, even though I hate it. I hate that I do it, and I hate that part of me feels relieved afterward. That’s why I’m here, because it’s starting to scare me how much I feel like I need to do it.

Yes… definitely. It’s almost always about that, even if I don’t admit it to myself right away.

Like, if I step on the scale and it’s up even a little bit — even a tiny amount — my whole mood just drops. I know that probably sounds dramatic, but in the moment it feels so real. Like I can’t just sit with it. That fear gets so loud that throwing up feels like the only way to make it quiet down.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I do. I mean, I’ll tell myself I’m just “being careful” or “getting back on track,” but really it’s because I’m scared of gaining weight.

Like if I feel like I ate too much the day before, the next day I’ll try not to eat until really late. I’ll drink coffee or water and keep telling myself I can wait a little longer, even if I’m shaky or my stomach is making noise. At work it’s a little easier to pretend I’m just busy, because I’m on my feet at the cleaners all day and there’s always something to fold or tag or press, but in my head I’m counting the hours and thinking about what I’m allowed to have later.

And sometimes I try to make up for eating by walking a lot or exercising, even when I’m already exhausted. My legs will hurt from standing all day, like actually ache, but I’ll still make myself keep moving because I feel like I have to undo what I ate. It’s not really about feeling healthy. It feels more like punishment, I guess. Like I don’t deserve to just eat normally if I messed up.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… yes, that happens. I hate saying that out loud, but it does.

It’s like once I start eating, something just… switches off, or maybe switches on, I don’t know. I can be telling myself the whole time, “Stop, just stop now, put it away,” and then my hands are still reaching for more food anyway. It feels almost like I’m watching myself do it from the outside, like I know what I’m doing and I know I’m going to feel awful about it, but I can’t make myself quit in that moment.

And it’s not like I’m enjoying it the way you’re supposed to enjoy food. At first maybe there’s this relief, like I finally get to eat, but then very quickly it turns into this panicky thing where I’m just grabbing and chewing and thinking, “Why am I still doing this?” But I keep going. Even if I feel full, even if I’m embarrassed, even if I’m promising myself every few seconds that this is the last bite. It doesn’t feel controlled. It feels like my body is moving faster than my brain can stop it.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… mostly, yes. I mean, I tell myself different things in the moment, like I’m just trying to feel less full or make the panic stop, but underneath it, it’s about weight. It’s about feeling like I ruined everything by eating and I have to get it out of me before it “counts,” if that makes sense.

That’s actually a big part of why I’m here. I keep making myself throw up after I eat, and it’s gotten to where it doesn’t even feel like a choice sometimes. I’ll eat and then my mind goes straight to, “You have to fix this.” Sometimes I do it in the bathroom at work, which makes me feel so ashamed because I’m trying to be quiet and normal and then go back out like nothing happened. Or I’ll hold it together until I get home and then go straight to the bathroom.

And I hate saying this, but it’s started to feel like the only way I can calm down after eating. Like I know it’s not okay, I know it’s disgusting and scary, but in that moment the fear is so loud that throwing up feels like the only thing that quiets it for a little while.

At work it’s a little easier to pretend I’m just busy, because I’m on my feet at the cleaners all day and there’s always something to fold or tag or press, but in my head I’m counting the hours and thinking about what I’m allowed to have later.\n\nAnd sometimes I try to make up for eating by walking a lot or exercising, even when I’m already exhausted. My legs will hurt from standing all day, like actually ache, but I’ll still make myself keep moving because I feel like I have to undo what I ate. It’s not really about feeling healthy. It feels more like punishment, I guess. Like I don’t deserve to just eat normally if I messed up.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that. I mean, I check my weight a lot, so I notice every little change, but I don’t know if it would count as “significant” the way you mean it.\n\nThere are times it goes down and I feel almost… relieved, like I did something right, and then if it goes up even a little I get really upset. But I can’t say I’ve had some big obvious weight loss that other people are commenting on or anything. At least, not that I can tell. I think I’m more just constantly watching it and reacting to every small number, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time, like within an hour or two, and feel like you can't stop or control how much you're eating?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that happens. I hate saying that out loud, but it does.\n\nIt’s like once I start eating, something just… switches off, or maybe switches on, I don’t know. I can be telling myself the whole time, “Stop, just stop now, put it away,” and then my hands are still reaching for more food anyway. It feels almost like I’m watching myself do it from the outside, like I know what I’m doing and I know I’m going to feel awful about it, but I can’t make myself quit in that moment.\n\nAnd it’s not like I’m enjoying it the way you’re supposed to enjoy food. At first maybe there’s this relief, like I finally get to eat, but then very quickly it turns into this panicky thing where I’m just grabbing and chewing and thinking, “Why am I still doing this?” But I keep going. Even if I feel full, even if I’m embarrassed, even if I’m promising myself every few seconds that this is the last bite. It doesn’t feel controlled. It feels like my body is moving faster than my brain can stop it.\n\nDoctor: Is making yourself throw up mostly about trying to control your weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly, yes. I mean, I tell myself different things in the moment, like I’m just trying to feel less full or make the panic stop, but underneath it, it’s about weight. It’s about feeling like I ruined everything by eating and I have to get it out of me before it “counts,” if that makes sense.\n\nThat’s actually a big part of why I’m here. I keep making myself throw up after I eat, and it’s gotten to where it doesn’t even feel like a choice sometimes. I’ll eat and then my mind goes straight to, “You have to fix this.” Sometimes I do it in the bathroom at work, which makes me feel so ashamed because I’m trying to be quiet and normal and then go back out like nothing happened. Or I’ll hold it together until I get home and then go straight to the bathroom.\n\nAnd I hate saying this, but it’s started to feel like the only way I can calm down after eating. Like I know it’s not okay, I know it’s disgusting and scary, but in that moment the fear is so loud that throwing up feels like the only thing that quiets it for a little while.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. For months now, it’s been happening most weeks… not every single day, but usually at least a couple times in a week. Sometimes more, depending on how things are going.

It’s usually like I eat way more than I meant to in a short amount of time, and then afterward I get this awful panic, like I have to undo it somehow. A lot of the time that means making myself throw up, but sometimes I’ll try not eating for a while after, or I’ll exercise because I feel like I have to burn it off. It’s not just once in a while anymore. It’s been this pattern for months, and I keep telling myself it was the last time, but then the next week it happens again.

But I can’t say I’ve had some big obvious weight loss that other people are commenting on or anything. At least, not that I can tell. I think I’m more just constantly watching it and reacting to every small number, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time, like within an hour or two, and feel like you can't stop or control how much you're eating?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that happens. I hate saying that out loud, but it does.\n\nIt’s like once I start eating, something just… switches off, or maybe switches on, I don’t know. I can be telling myself the whole time, “Stop, just stop now, put it away,” and then my hands are still reaching for more food anyway. It feels almost like I’m watching myself do it from the outside, like I know what I’m doing and I know I’m going to feel awful about it, but I can’t make myself quit in that moment.\n\nAnd it’s not like I’m enjoying it the way you’re supposed to enjoy food. At first maybe there’s this relief, like I finally get to eat, but then very quickly it turns into this panicky thing where I’m just grabbing and chewing and thinking, “Why am I still doing this?” But I keep going. Even if I feel full, even if I’m embarrassed, even if I’m promising myself every few seconds that this is the last bite. It doesn’t feel controlled. It feels like my body is moving faster than my brain can stop it.\n\nDoctor: Is making yourself throw up mostly about trying to control your weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly, yes. I mean, I tell myself different things in the moment, like I’m just trying to feel less full or make the panic stop, but underneath it, it’s about weight. It’s about feeling like I ruined everything by eating and I have to get it out of me before it “counts,” if that makes sense.\n\nThat’s actually a big part of why I’m here. I keep making myself throw up after I eat, and it’s gotten to where it doesn’t even feel like a choice sometimes. I’ll eat and then my mind goes straight to, “You have to fix this.” Sometimes I do it in the bathroom at work, which makes me feel so ashamed because I’m trying to be quiet and normal and then go back out like nothing happened. Or I’ll hold it together until I get home and then go straight to the bathroom.\n\nAnd I hate saying this, but it’s started to feel like the only way I can calm down after eating. Like I know it’s not okay, I know it’s disgusting and scary, but in that moment the fear is so loud that throwing up feels like the only thing that quiets it for a little while.\n\nDoctor: How often do you have episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: For months now, it’s been happening most weeks… not every single day, but usually at least a couple times in a week. Sometimes more, depending on how things are going.\n\nIt’s usually like I eat way more than I meant to in a short amount of time, and then afterward I get this awful panic, like I have to undo it somehow. A lot of the time that means making myself throw up, but sometimes I’ll try not eating for a while after, or I’ll exercise because I feel like I have to burn it off. It’s not just once in a while anymore. It’s been this pattern for months, and I keep telling myself it was the last time, but then the next week it happens again.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It’s… it’s often. More often than I want to admit.

A lot of the time after I eat, especially if I feel like I ate too much, my mind goes straight to needing to throw up. Sometimes I’ll do it right away, like if I’m at work I’ll go into the bathroom and try to be quiet and act normal afterward, which makes me feel horrible. Other times I’ll hold it in until I get home, and then it’s like the first thing I do is go to the bathroom because I can’t calm down until I’ve done it.

It’s not every single time I eat, but it’s become… I don’t know, this regular thing. Like after eating, if that panic starts, throwing up feels like the only way I can make it stop. And I hate that. I hate that it feels like the thing I rely on now, because I know it’s not normal and I feel so ashamed afterward, but in the moment I feel desperate.

At work it’s a little easier to pretend I’m just busy, because I’m on my feet at the cleaners all day and there’s always something to fold or tag or press, but in my head I’m counting the hours and thinking about what I’m allowed to have later.\n\nAnd sometimes I try to make up for eating by walking a lot or exercising, even when I’m already exhausted. My legs will hurt from standing all day, like actually ache, but I’ll still make myself keep moving because I feel like I have to undo what I ate. It’s not really about feeling healthy. It feels more like punishment, I guess. Like I don’t deserve to just eat normally if I messed up.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that. I mean, I check my weight a lot, so I notice every little change, but I don’t know if it would count as “significant” the way you mean it.\n\nThere are times it goes down and I feel almost… relieved, like I did something right, and then if it goes up even a little I get really upset. But I can’t say I’ve had some big obvious weight loss that other people are commenting on or anything. At least, not that I can tell. I think I’m more just constantly watching it and reacting to every small number, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time, like within an hour or two, and feel like you can't stop or control how much you're eating?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that happens. I hate saying that out loud, but it does.\n\nIt’s like once I start eating, something just… switches off, or maybe switches on, I don’t know. I can be telling myself the whole time, “Stop, just stop now, put it away,” and then my hands are still reaching for more food anyway. It feels almost like I’m watching myself do it from the outside, like I know what I’m doing and I know I’m going to feel awful about it, but I can’t make myself quit in that moment.\n\nAnd it’s not like I’m enjoying it the way you’re supposed to enjoy food. At first maybe there’s this relief, like I finally get to eat, but then very quickly it turns into this panicky thing where I’m just grabbing and chewing and thinking, “Why am I still doing this?” But I keep going. Even if I feel full, even if I’m embarrassed, even if I’m promising myself every few seconds that this is the last bite. It doesn’t feel controlled. It feels like my body is moving faster than my brain can stop it.\n\nDoctor: Is making yourself throw up mostly about trying to control your weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly, yes. I mean, I tell myself different things in the moment, like I’m just trying to feel less full or make the panic stop, but underneath it, it’s about weight. It’s about feeling like I ruined everything by eating and I have to get it out of me before it “counts,” if that makes sense.\n\nThat’s actually a big part of why I’m here. I keep making myself throw up after I eat, and it’s gotten to where it doesn’t even feel like a choice sometimes. I’ll eat and then my mind goes straight to, “You have to fix this.” Sometimes I do it in the bathroom at work, which makes me feel so ashamed because I’m trying to be quiet and normal and then go back out like nothing happened. Or I’ll hold it together until I get home and then go straight to the bathroom.\n\nAnd I hate saying this, but it’s started to feel like the only way I can calm down after eating. Like I know it’s not okay, I know it’s disgusting and scary, but in that moment the fear is so loud that throwing up feels like the only thing that quiets it for a little while.\n\nDoctor: How often do you have episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: For months now, it’s been happening most weeks… not every single day, but usually at least a couple times in a week. Sometimes more, depending on how things are going.\n\nIt’s usually like I eat way more than I meant to in a short amount of time, and then afterward I get this awful panic, like I have to undo it somehow. A lot of the time that means making myself throw up, but sometimes I’ll try not eating for a while after, or I’ll exercise because I feel like I have to burn it off. It’s not just once in a while anymore. It’s been this pattern for months, and I keep telling myself it was the last time, but then the next week it happens again.\n\nDoctor: How often do you make yourself throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s often. More often than I want to admit.\n\nA lot of the time after I eat, especially if I feel like I ate too much, my mind goes straight to needing to throw up. Sometimes I’ll do it right away, like if I’m at work I’ll go into the bathroom and try to be quiet and act normal afterward, which makes me feel horrible. Other times I’ll hold it in until I get home, and then it’s like the first thing I do is go to the bathroom because I can’t calm down until I’ve done it.\n\nIt’s not every single time I eat, but it’s become… I don’t know, this regular thing. Like after eating, if that panic starts, throwing up feels like the only way I can make it stop. And I hate that. I hate that it feels like the thing I rely on now, because I know it’s not normal and I feel so ashamed afterward, but in the moment I feel desperate.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Sometimes I do it in the bathroom at work, like if I’ve eaten and then I start feeling panicky or just wrong in my body, and sometimes I wait until I get home and go straight to the bathroom.\n\nIt’s gotten to where it feels like the only thing that calms me down, even though I hate it. I hate that I do it, and I hate that part of me feels relieved afterward. That’s why I’m here, because it’s starting to scare me how much I feel like I need to do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about your weight when you feel the need to throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely. It’s almost always about that, even if I don’t admit it to myself right away.\n\nLike, if I step on the scale and it’s up even a little bit — even a tiny amount — my whole mood just drops. I start panicking like I’ve ruined everything, like whatever I ate has already changed my body. Or if I’m at work and my waistband feels tight under my apron, I can’t stop thinking about it. I keep noticing it every time I bend or move, and it’s like this alarm going off in my head that I’m going to be fat by the end of the day if I don’t do something.\n\nI know that probably sounds dramatic, but in the moment it feels so real. Like I can’t just sit with it. That fear gets so loud that throwing up feels like the only way to make it quiet down.\n\nDoctor: Do you often limit the amount or type of food you eat to try to control your weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, I’ll tell myself I’m just “being careful” or “getting back on track,” but really it’s because I’m scared of gaining weight.\n\nLike if I feel like I ate too much the day before, the next day I’ll try not to eat until really late. I’ll drink coffee or water and keep telling myself I can wait a little longer, even if I’m shaky or my stomach is making noise. At work it’s a little easier to pretend I’m just busy, because I’m on my feet at the cleaners all day and there’s always something to fold or tag or press, but in my head I’m counting the hours and thinking about what I’m allowed to have later.\n\nAnd sometimes I try to make up for eating by walking a lot or exercising, even when I’m already exhausted. My legs will hurt from standing all day, like actually ache, but I’ll still make myself keep moving because I feel like I have to undo what I ate. It’s not really about feeling healthy. It feels more like punishment, I guess. Like I don’t deserve to just eat normally if I messed up.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that. I mean, I check my weight a lot, so I notice every little change, but I don’t know if it would count as “significant” the way you mean it.\n\nThere are times it goes down and I feel almost… relieved, like I did something right, and then if it goes up even a little I get really upset. But I can’t say I’ve had some big obvious weight loss that other people are commenting on or anything. At least, not that I can tell. I think I’m more just constantly watching it and reacting to every small number, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time, like within an hour or two, and feel like you can't stop or control how much you're eating?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that happens. I hate saying that out loud, but it does.\n\nIt’s like once I start eating, something just… switches off, or maybe switches on, I don’t know. I can be telling myself the whole time, “Stop, just stop now, put it away,” and then my hands are still reaching for more food anyway. It feels almost like I’m watching myself do it from the outside, like I know what I’m doing and I know I’m going to feel awful about it, but I can’t make myself quit in that moment.\n\nAnd it’s not like I’m enjoying it the way you’re supposed to enjoy food. At first maybe there’s this relief, like I finally get to eat, but then very quickly it turns into this panicky thing where I’m just grabbing and chewing and thinking, “Why am I still doing this?” But I keep going. Even if I feel full, even if I’m embarrassed, even if I’m promising myself every few seconds that this is the last bite. It doesn’t feel controlled. It feels like my body is moving faster than my brain can stop it.\n\nDoctor: Is making yourself throw up mostly about trying to control your weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly, yes. I mean, I tell myself different things in the moment, like I’m just trying to feel less full or make the panic stop, but underneath it, it’s about weight. It’s about feeling like I ruined everything by eating and I have to get it out of me before it “counts,” if that makes sense.\n\nThat’s actually a big part of why I’m here. I keep making myself throw up after I eat, and it’s gotten to where it doesn’t even feel like a choice sometimes. I’ll eat and then my mind goes straight to, “You have to fix this.” Sometimes I do it in the bathroom at work, which makes me feel so ashamed because I’m trying to be quiet and normal and then go back out like nothing happened. Or I’ll hold it together until I get home and then go straight to the bathroom.\n\nAnd I hate saying this, but it’s started to feel like the only way I can calm down after eating. Like I know it’s not okay, I know it’s disgusting and scary, but in that moment the fear is so loud that throwing up feels like the only thing that quiets it for a little while.\n\nDoctor: How often do you have episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: For months now, it’s been happening most weeks… not every single day, but usually at least a couple times in a week. Sometimes more, depending on how things are going.\n\nIt’s usually like I eat way more than I meant to in a short amount of time, and then afterward I get this awful panic, like I have to undo it somehow. A lot of the time that means making myself throw up, but sometimes I’ll try not eating for a while after, or I’ll exercise because I feel like I have to burn it off. It’s not just once in a while anymore. It’s been this pattern for months, and I keep telling myself it was the last time, but then the next week it happens again.\n\nDoctor: How often do you make yourself throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s often. More often than I want to admit.\n\nA lot of the time after I eat, especially if I feel like I ate too much, my mind goes straight to needing to throw up. Sometimes I’ll do it right away, like if I’m at work I’ll go into the bathroom and try to be quiet and act normal afterward, which makes me feel horrible. Other times I’ll hold it in until I get home, and then it’s like the first thing I do is go to the bathroom because I can’t calm down until I’ve done it.\n\nIt’s not every single time I eat, but it’s become… I don’t know, this regular thing. Like after eating, if that panic starts, throwing up feels like the only way I can make it stop. And I hate that. I hate that it feels like the thing I rely on now, because I know it’s not normal and I feel so ashamed afterward, but in the moment I feel desperate.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your clothes fitting differently due to weight loss?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean… I pay attention to my body a lot, probably too much, but with my clothes it’s hard for me to say if they’re actually fitting differently or if I’m just feeling different in them.\n\nSome days I put something on and I’m convinced it feels looser or tighter, but then I don’t know if that’s real or if I’m just checking and overthinking it. I haven’t had, like, a clear moment where all my clothes are suddenly falling off me or anything like that. I just… I don’t trust my own sense of it very much.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not every single time, no… but a lot of the time, yes. Enough that it feels like that’s the pattern now.

If I have one of those episodes where I eat a lot and I feel like I went too far, it’s like my head immediately starts screaming at me that I have to get it out. Sometimes I try to sit with it for a little while, or I tell myself I’m not going to do it this time, but then I get so worked up and panicky that I end up going to the bathroom anyway.

And it’s not just at home, which is part of what scares me. I’ve done it in the bathroom at work, trying to be quiet and then come back out like nothing happened. Other times I wait until I get home, but then it’s almost like I can’t relax or think straight until I’ve thrown up. I hate saying that, because it sounds awful, but it’s started feeling like the only way I can calm down after I eat like that.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.2, F50.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… the main thing is that I keep making myself throw up after I eat. It’s hard to say out loud, honestly. Sometimes I do it in the bathroom at work, like if I’ve eaten and then I start feeling panicky or just wrong in my body, and sometimes I wait until I get home and go straight to the bathroom.\n\nIt’s gotten to where it feels like the only thing that calms me down, even though I hate it. I hate that I do it, and I hate that part of me feels relieved afterward. That’s why I’m here, because it’s starting to scare me how much I feel like I need to do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about your weight when you feel the need to throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely. It’s almost always about that, even if I don’t admit it to myself right away.\n\nLike, if I step on the scale and it’s up even a little bit — even a tiny amount — my whole mood just drops. I start panicking like I’ve ruined everything, like whatever I ate has already changed my body. Or if I’m at work and my waistband feels tight under my apron, I can’t stop thinking about it. I keep noticing it every time I bend or move, and it’s like this alarm going off in my head that I’m going to be fat by the end of the day if I don’t do something.\n\nI know that probably sounds dramatic, but in the moment it feels so real. Like I can’t just sit with it. That fear gets so loud that throwing up feels like the only way to make it quiet down.\n\nDoctor: Do you often limit the amount or type of food you eat to try to control your weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, I’ll tell myself I’m just “being careful” or “getting back on track,” but really it’s because I’m scared of gaining weight.\n\nLike if I feel like I ate too much the day before, the next day I’ll try not to eat until really late. I’ll drink coffee or water and keep telling myself I can wait a little longer, even if I’m shaky or my stomach is making noise. At work it’s a little easier to pretend I’m just busy, because I’m on my feet at the cleaners all day and there’s always something to fold or tag or press, but in my head I’m counting the hours and thinking about what I’m allowed to have later.\n\nAnd sometimes I try to make up for eating by walking a lot or exercising, even when I’m already exhausted. My legs will hurt from standing all day, like actually ache, but I’ll still make myself keep moving because I feel like I have to undo what I ate. It’s not really about feeling healthy. It feels more like punishment, I guess. Like I don’t deserve to just eat normally if I messed up.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that. I mean, I check my weight a lot, so I notice every little change, but I don’t know if it would count as “significant” the way you mean it.\n\nThere are times it goes down and I feel almost… relieved, like I did something right, and then if it goes up even a little I get really upset. But I can’t say I’ve had some big obvious weight loss that other people are commenting on or anything. At least, not that I can tell. I think I’m more just constantly watching it and reacting to every small number, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time, like within an hour or two, and feel like you can't stop or control how much you're eating?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that happens. I hate saying that out loud, but it does.\n\nIt’s like once I start eating, something just… switches off, or maybe switches on, I don’t know. I can be telling myself the whole time, “Stop, just stop now, put it away,” and then my hands are still reaching for more food anyway. It feels almost like I’m watching myself do it from the outside, like I know what I’m doing and I know I’m going to feel awful about it, but I can’t make myself quit in that moment.\n\nAnd it’s not like I’m enjoying it the way you’re supposed to enjoy food. At first maybe there’s this relief, like I finally get to eat, but then very quickly it turns into this panicky thing where I’m just grabbing and chewing and thinking, “Why am I still doing this?” But I keep going. Even if I feel full, even if I’m embarrassed, even if I’m promising myself every few seconds that this is the last bite. It doesn’t feel controlled. It feels like my body is moving faster than my brain can stop it.\n\nDoctor: Is making yourself throw up mostly about trying to control your weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly, yes. I mean, I tell myself different things in the moment, like I’m just trying to feel less full or make the panic stop, but underneath it, it’s about weight. It’s about feeling like I ruined everything by eating and I have to get it out of me before it “counts,” if that makes sense.\n\nThat’s actually a big part of why I’m here. I keep making myself throw up after I eat, and it’s gotten to where it doesn’t even feel like a choice sometimes. I’ll eat and then my mind goes straight to, “You have to fix this.” Sometimes I do it in the bathroom at work, which makes me feel so ashamed because I’m trying to be quiet and normal and then go back out like nothing happened. Or I’ll hold it together until I get home and then go straight to the bathroom.\n\nAnd I hate saying this, but it’s started to feel like the only way I can calm down after eating. Like I know it’s not okay, I know it’s disgusting and scary, but in that moment the fear is so loud that throwing up feels like the only thing that quiets it for a little while.\n\nDoctor: How often do you have episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: For months now, it’s been happening most weeks… not every single day, but usually at least a couple times in a week. Sometimes more, depending on how things are going.\n\nIt’s usually like I eat way more than I meant to in a short amount of time, and then afterward I get this awful panic, like I have to undo it somehow. A lot of the time that means making myself throw up, but sometimes I’ll try not eating for a while after, or I’ll exercise because I feel like I have to burn it off. It’s not just once in a while anymore. It’s been this pattern for months, and I keep telling myself it was the last time, but then the next week it happens again.\n\nDoctor: How often do you make yourself throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s often. More often than I want to admit.\n\nA lot of the time after I eat, especially if I feel like I ate too much, my mind goes straight to needing to throw up. Sometimes I’ll do it right away, like if I’m at work I’ll go into the bathroom and try to be quiet and act normal afterward, which makes me feel horrible. Other times I’ll hold it in until I get home, and then it’s like the first thing I do is go to the bathroom because I can’t calm down until I’ve done it.\n\nIt’s not every single time I eat, but it’s become… I don’t know, this regular thing. Like after eating, if that panic starts, throwing up feels like the only way I can make it stop. And I hate that. I hate that it feels like the thing I rely on now, because I know it’s not normal and I feel so ashamed afterward, but in the moment I feel desperate.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your clothes fitting differently due to weight loss?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean… I pay attention to my body a lot, probably too much, but with my clothes it’s hard for me to say if they’re actually fitting differently or if I’m just feeling different in them.\n\nSome days I put something on and I’m convinced it feels looser or tighter, but then I don’t know if that’s real or if I’m just checking and overthinking it. I haven’t had, like, a clear moment where all my clothes are suddenly falling off me or anything like that. I just… I don’t trust my own sense of it very much.\n\nDoctor: Do you make yourself throw up after every episode of eating a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: Not every single time, no… but a lot of the time, yes. Enough that it feels like that’s the pattern now.\n\nIf I have one of those episodes where I eat a lot and I feel like I went too far, it’s like my head immediately starts screaming at me that I have to get it out. Sometimes I try to sit with it for a little while, or I tell myself I’m not going to do it this time, but then I get so worked up and panicky that I end up going to the bathroom anyway.\n\nAnd it’s not just at home, which is part of what scares me. I’ve done it in the bathroom at work, trying to be quiet and then come back out like nothing happened. Other times I wait until I get home, but then it’s almost like I can’t relax or think straight until I’ve thrown up. I hate saying that, because it sounds awful, but it’s started feeling like the only way I can calm down after I eat like that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I’ve done it in the bathroom at work, trying to be quiet and then come back out like nothing happened. Other times I wait until I get home, but then it’s almost like I can’t relax or think straight until I’ve thrown up. I hate saying that, because it sounds awful, but it’s started feeling like the only way I can calm down after I eat like that.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you always feel relieved after making yourself throw up?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Sometimes I’ll do it right away, like if I’m at work I’ll go into the bathroom and try to be quiet and act normal afterward, which makes me feel horrible. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not always… I mean, at first there’s usually this kind of relief, like the panic drops down a little because I did the thing my mind was telling me I had to do. That’s the part that scares me, honestly, because it’s started feeling like the only way I can calm down after I eat.

But it’s not a good relief. It’s not like I feel okay afterward. A lot of times I feel ashamed right away, or tired, or like I can’t believe I did it again. If I’m at work and I’ve gone into the bathroom to make myself throw up, then I have to come back out and act normal, like nothing happened, and that feels awful. And if I’ve waited until I get home, sometimes I’m just rushing straight to the bathroom because I’ve been holding all that panic in, and then afterward I’m relieved but also upset with myself.

So… yes and no. It calms something down in the moment, but then I hate that I needed it to feel calm.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.2, F50.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… the main thing is that I keep making myself throw up after I eat. It’s hard to say out loud, honestly. Sometimes I do it in the bathroom at work, like if I’ve eaten and then I start feeling panicky or just wrong in my body, and sometimes I wait until I get home and go straight to the bathroom.\n\nIt’s gotten to where it feels like the only thing that calms me down, even though I hate it. I hate that I do it, and I hate that part of me feels relieved afterward. That’s why I’m here, because it’s starting to scare me how much I feel like I need to do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about your weight when you feel the need to throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely. It’s almost always about that, even if I don’t admit it to myself right away.\n\nLike, if I step on the scale and it’s up even a little bit — even a tiny amount — my whole mood just drops. I start panicking like I’ve ruined everything, like whatever I ate has already changed my body. Or if I’m at work and my waistband feels tight under my apron, I can’t stop thinking about it. I keep noticing it every time I bend or move, and it’s like this alarm going off in my head that I’m going to be fat by the end of the day if I don’t do something.\n\nI know that probably sounds dramatic, but in the moment it feels so real. Like I can’t just sit with it. That fear gets so loud that throwing up feels like the only way to make it quiet down.\n\nDoctor: Do you often limit the amount or type of food you eat to try to control your weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, I’ll tell myself I’m just “being careful” or “getting back on track,” but really it’s because I’m scared of gaining weight.\n\nLike if I feel like I ate too much the day before, the next day I’ll try not to eat until really late. I’ll drink coffee or water and keep telling myself I can wait a little longer, even if I’m shaky or my stomach is making noise. At work it’s a little easier to pretend I’m just busy, because I’m on my feet at the cleaners all day and there’s always something to fold or tag or press, but in my head I’m counting the hours and thinking about what I’m allowed to have later.\n\nAnd sometimes I try to make up for eating by walking a lot or exercising, even when I’m already exhausted. My legs will hurt from standing all day, like actually ache, but I’ll still make myself keep moving because I feel like I have to undo what I ate. It’s not really about feeling healthy. It feels more like punishment, I guess. Like I don’t deserve to just eat normally if I messed up.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that. I mean, I check my weight a lot, so I notice every little change, but I don’t know if it would count as “significant” the way you mean it.\n\nThere are times it goes down and I feel almost… relieved, like I did something right, and then if it goes up even a little I get really upset. But I can’t say I’ve had some big obvious weight loss that other people are commenting on or anything. At least, not that I can tell. I think I’m more just constantly watching it and reacting to every small number, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time, like within an hour or two, and feel like you can't stop or control how much you're eating?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that happens. I hate saying that out loud, but it does.\n\nIt’s like once I start eating, something just… switches off, or maybe switches on, I don’t know. I can be telling myself the whole time, “Stop, just stop now, put it away,” and then my hands are still reaching for more food anyway. It feels almost like I’m watching myself do it from the outside, like I know what I’m doing and I know I’m going to feel awful about it, but I can’t make myself quit in that moment.\n\nAnd it’s not like I’m enjoying it the way you’re supposed to enjoy food. At first maybe there’s this relief, like I finally get to eat, but then very quickly it turns into this panicky thing where I’m just grabbing and chewing and thinking, “Why am I still doing this?” But I keep going. Even if I feel full, even if I’m embarrassed, even if I’m promising myself every few seconds that this is the last bite. It doesn’t feel controlled. It feels like my body is moving faster than my brain can stop it.\n\nDoctor: Is making yourself throw up mostly about trying to control your weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly, yes. I mean, I tell myself different things in the moment, like I’m just trying to feel less full or make the panic stop, but underneath it, it’s about weight. It’s about feeling like I ruined everything by eating and I have to get it out of me before it “counts,” if that makes sense.\n\nThat’s actually a big part of why I’m here. I keep making myself throw up after I eat, and it’s gotten to where it doesn’t even feel like a choice sometimes. I’ll eat and then my mind goes straight to, “You have to fix this.” Sometimes I do it in the bathroom at work, which makes me feel so ashamed because I’m trying to be quiet and normal and then go back out like nothing happened. Or I’ll hold it together until I get home and then go straight to the bathroom.\n\nAnd I hate saying this, but it’s started to feel like the only way I can calm down after eating. Like I know it’s not okay, I know it’s disgusting and scary, but in that moment the fear is so loud that throwing up feels like the only thing that quiets it for a little while.\n\nDoctor: How often do you have episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: For months now, it’s been happening most weeks… not every single day, but usually at least a couple times in a week. Sometimes more, depending on how things are going.\n\nIt’s usually like I eat way more than I meant to in a short amount of time, and then afterward I get this awful panic, like I have to undo it somehow. A lot of the time that means making myself throw up, but sometimes I’ll try not eating for a while after, or I’ll exercise because I feel like I have to burn it off. It’s not just once in a while anymore. It’s been this pattern for months, and I keep telling myself it was the last time, but then the next week it happens again.\n\nDoctor: How often do you make yourself throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s often. More often than I want to admit.\n\nA lot of the time after I eat, especially if I feel like I ate too much, my mind goes straight to needing to throw up. Sometimes I’ll do it right away, like if I’m at work I’ll go into the bathroom and try to be quiet and act normal afterward, which makes me feel horrible. Other times I’ll hold it in until I get home, and then it’s like the first thing I do is go to the bathroom because I can’t calm down until I’ve done it.\n\nIt’s not every single time I eat, but it’s become… I don’t know, this regular thing. I just… I don’t trust my own sense of it very much.\n\nDoctor: Do you make yourself throw up after every episode of eating a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: Not every single time, no… but a lot of the time, yes. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s usually at least a couple times a week… I mean, most weeks. I don’t always write it down or anything, so I’m trying to be honest without giving you some exact number that might not be right, but for months now it’s been happening regularly.

Like, there will be these episodes where I eat way more than I meant to in a short time, and afterward I feel like I have to fix it somehow. A lot of the time that means throwing up, but sometimes I’ll try to make up for it by not eating for a while, or by exercising like I’m trying to burn it off. It kind of depends on where I am and how panicked I feel.

But it’s not just once in a while anymore. Most weeks, there are at least two times where it happens, sometimes more.

I’ll drink coffee or water and keep telling myself I can wait a little longer, even if I’m shaky or my stomach is making noise. My legs will hurt from standing all day, like actually ache, but I’ll still make myself keep moving because I feel like I have to undo what I ate. It’s not really about feeling healthy. It feels more like punishment, I guess. Like I don’t deserve to just eat normally if I messed up.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that. But I can’t say I’ve had some big obvious weight loss that other people are commenting on or anything. At least, not that I can tell. I think I’m more just constantly watching it and reacting to every small number, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time, like within an hour or two, and feel like you can't stop or control how much you're eating?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that happens. I hate saying that out loud, but it does.\n\nIt’s like once I start eating, something just… switches off, or maybe switches on, I don’t know. I can be telling myself the whole time, “Stop, just stop now, put it away,” and then my hands are still reaching for more food anyway. It feels almost like I’m watching myself do it from the outside, like I know what I’m doing and I know I’m going to feel awful about it, but I can’t make myself quit in that moment.\n\nAnd it’s not like I’m enjoying it the way you’re supposed to enjoy food. I mean, I tell myself different things in the moment, like I’m just trying to feel less full or make the panic stop, but underneath it, it’s about weight. It’s about feeling like I ruined everything by eating and I have to get it out of me before it “counts,” if that makes sense.\n\nThat’s actually a big part of why I’m here. I’ll eat and then my mind goes straight to, “You have to fix this.” Sometimes I do it in the bathroom at work, which makes me feel so ashamed because I’m trying to be quiet and normal and then go back out like nothing happened. Or I’ll hold it together until I get home and then go straight to the bathroom.\n\nAnd I hate saying this, but it’s started to feel like the only way I can calm down after eating. Like I know it’s not okay, I know it’s disgusting and scary, but in that moment the fear is so loud that throwing up feels like the only thing that quiets it for a little while.\n\nDoctor: How often do you have episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: For months now, it’s been happening most weeks… not every single day, but usually at least a couple times in a week. It’s not just once in a while anymore. I mean, I’ll tell myself I’m just “being careful” or “getting back on track,” but really it’s because I’m scared of gaining weight.\n\nLike if I feel like I ate too much the day before, the next day I’ll try not to eat until really late. I’ll drink coffee or water and keep telling myself I can wait a little longer, even if I’m shaky or my stomach is making noise. At work it’s a little easier to pretend I’m just busy, because I’m on my feet at the cleaners all day and there’s always something to fold or tag or press, but in my head I’m counting the hours and thinking about what I’m allowed to have later.\n\nAnd sometimes I try to make up for eating by walking a lot or exercising, even when I’m already exhausted. My legs will hurt from standing all day, like actually ache, but I’ll still make myself keep moving because I feel like I have to undo what I ate. It’s not really about feeling healthy. It feels more like punishment, I guess. Like I don’t deserve to just eat normally if I messed up.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that. I mean, I check my weight a lot, so I notice every little change, but I don’t know if it would count as “significant” the way you mean it.\n\nThere are times it goes down and I feel almost… relieved, like I did something right, and then if it goes up even a little I get really upset. But I can’t say I’ve had some big obvious weight loss that other people are commenting on or anything. At least, not that I can tell. I think I’m more just constantly watching it and reacting to every small number, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time, like within an hour or two, and feel like you can't stop or control how much you're eating?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that happens. I hate saying that out loud, but it does.\n\nIt’s like once I start eating, something just… switches off, or maybe switches on, I don’t know. I can be telling myself the whole time, “Stop, just stop now, put it away,” and then my hands are still reaching for more food anyway. It feels almost like I’m watching myself do it from the outside, like I know what I’m doing and I know I’m going to feel awful about it, but I can’t make myself quit in that moment.\n\nAnd it’s not like I’m enjoying it the way you’re supposed to enjoy food. At first maybe there’s this relief, like I finally get to eat, but then very quickly it turns into this panicky thing where I’m just grabbing and chewing and thinking, “Why am I still doing this?” But I keep going. Even if I feel full, even if I’m embarrassed, even if I’m promising myself every few seconds that this is the last bite. It doesn’t feel controlled. It feels like my body is moving faster than my brain can stop it.\n\nDoctor: Is making yourself throw up mostly about trying to control your weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly, yes. I mean, I tell myself different things in the moment, like I’m just trying to feel less full or make the panic stop, but underneath it, it’s about weight. It’s about feeling like I ruined everything by eating and I have to get it out of me before it “counts,” if that makes sense.\n\nThat’s actually a big part of why I’m here. I keep making myself throw up after I eat, and it’s gotten to where it doesn’t even feel like a choice sometimes. I’ll eat and then my mind goes straight to, “You have to fix this.” Sometimes I do it in the bathroom at work, which makes me feel so ashamed because I’m trying to be quiet and normal and then go back out like nothing happened. Or I’ll hold it together until I get home and then go straight to the bathroom.\n\nAnd I hate saying this, but it’s started to feel like the only way I can calm down after eating. Like I know it’s not okay, I know it’s disgusting and scary, but in that moment the fear is so loud that throwing up feels like the only thing that quiets it for a little while.\n\nDoctor: How often do you have episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: For months now, it’s been happening most weeks… not every single day, but usually at least a couple times in a week. Sometimes more, depending on how things are going.\n\nIt’s usually like I eat way more than I meant to in a short amount of time, and then afterward I get this awful panic, like I have to undo it somehow. A lot of the time that means making myself throw up, but sometimes I’ll try not eating for a while after, or I’ll exercise because I feel like I have to burn it off. It’s not just once in a while anymore. It’s been this pattern for months, and I keep telling myself it was the last time, but then the next week it happens again.\n\nDoctor: How often do you make yourself throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s often. More often than I want to admit.\n\nA lot of the time after I eat, especially if I feel like I ate too much, my mind goes straight to needing to throw up. Sometimes I’ll do it right away, like if I’m at work I’ll go into the bathroom and try to be quiet and act normal afterward, which makes me feel horrible. Other times I’ll hold it in until I get home, and then it’s like the first thing I do is go to the bathroom because I can’t calm down until I’ve done it.\n\nIt’s not every single time I eat, but it’s become… I don’t know, this regular thing. Like after eating, if that panic starts, throwing up feels like the only way I can make it stop. And I hate that. I hate that it feels like the thing I rely on now, because I know it’s not normal and I feel so ashamed afterward, but in the moment I feel desperate.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your clothes fitting differently due to weight loss?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean… I pay attention to my body a lot, probably too much, but with my clothes it’s hard for me to say if they’re actually fitting differently or if I’m just feeling different in them.\n\nSome days I put something on and I’m convinced it feels looser or tighter, but then I don’t know if that’s real or if I’m just checking and overthinking it. I haven’t had, like, a clear moment where all my clothes are suddenly falling off me or anything like that. I just… I don’t trust my own sense of it very much.\n\nDoctor: Do you make yourself throw up after every episode of eating a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: Not every single time, no… but a lot of the time, yes. Enough that it feels like that’s the pattern now.\n\nIf I have one of those episodes where I eat a lot and I feel like I went too far, it’s like my head immediately starts screaming at me that I have to get it out. Sometimes I try to sit with it for a little while, or I tell myself I’m not going to do it this time, but then I get so worked up and panicky that I end up going to the bathroom anyway.\n\nAnd it’s not just at home, which is part of what scares me. I’ve done it in the bathroom at work, trying to be quiet and then come back out like nothing happened. Other times I wait until I get home, but then it’s almost like I can’t relax or think straight until I’ve thrown up. I hate saying that, because it sounds awful, but it’s started feeling like the only way I can calm down after I eat like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you always feel relieved after making yourself throw up?\n\nPatient: Not always… I mean, at first there’s usually this kind of relief, like the panic drops down a little because I did the thing my mind was telling me I had to do. That’s the part that scares me, honestly, because it’s started feeling like the only way I can calm down after I eat.\n\nBut it’s not a good relief. It’s not like I feel okay afterward. A lot of times I feel ashamed right away, or tired, or like I can’t believe I did it again. A lot of the time that means throwing up, but sometimes I’ll try to make up for it by not eating for a while, or by exercising like I’m trying to burn it off. Most weeks, there are at least two times where it happens, sometimes more.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Too often. I mean… I check it a lot, probably more than I should. Sometimes it’s like I tell myself I’m just going to look once, just to know, but then if the number is even a little higher than what I expected, I panic.

It can be the smallest change, and my brain treats it like it means everything is ruined. Like I’ve messed up completely and I’m going to just keep gaining and be fat by the end of the day, even though I know that probably sounds dramatic when I say it out loud. But in the moment it feels real.

And it’s not only the scale. At work, if my waistband feels tight under my apron, I start focusing on it and I can’t stop. I’ll keep thinking about whether I look bigger, whether other people can tell, whether I’ve let myself go. It makes me feel trapped in my own body. So… I don’t know the exact number, but I check my weight and my body a lot because I’m scared of it going up.

I keep noticing it every time I bend or move, and it’s like this alarm going off in my head that I’m going to be fat by the end of the day if I don’t do something.\n\nI know that probably sounds dramatic, but in the moment it feels so real. Like I can’t just sit with it. That fear gets so loud that throwing up feels like the only way to make it quiet down.\n\nDoctor: Do you often limit the amount or type of food you eat to try to control your weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I’ll drink coffee or water and keep telling myself I can wait a little longer, even if I’m shaky or my stomach is making noise. At work it’s a little easier to pretend I’m just busy, because I’m on my feet at the cleaners all day and there’s always something to fold or tag or press, but in my head I’m counting the hours and thinking about what I’m allowed to have later.\n\nAnd sometimes I try to make up for eating by walking a lot or exercising, even when I’m already exhausted. My legs will hurt from standing all day, like actually ache, but I’ll still make myself keep moving because I feel like I have to undo what I ate. It’s not really about feeling healthy. It feels more like punishment, I guess. Like I don’t deserve to just eat normally if I messed up.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that. I mean, I check my weight a lot, so I notice every little change, but I don’t know if it would count as “significant” the way you mean it.\n\nThere are times it goes down and I feel almost… relieved, like I did something right, and then if it goes up even a little I get really upset. But I can’t say I’ve had some big obvious weight loss that other people are commenting on or anything. At least, not that I can tell. I think I’m more just constantly watching it and reacting to every small number, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time, like within an hour or two, and feel like you can't stop or control how much you're eating?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that happens. I hate saying that out loud, but it does.\n\nIt’s like once I start eating, something just… switches off, or maybe switches on, I don’t know. I can be telling myself the whole time, “Stop, just stop now, put it away,” and then my hands are still reaching for more food anyway. It feels almost like I’m watching myself do it from the outside, like I know what I’m doing and I know I’m going to feel awful about it, but I can’t make myself quit in that moment.\n\nAnd it’s not like I’m enjoying it the way you’re supposed to enjoy food. At first maybe there’s this relief, like I finally get to eat, but then very quickly it turns into this panicky thing where I’m just grabbing and chewing and thinking, “Why am I still doing this?” But I keep going. Even if I feel full, even if I’m embarrassed, even if I’m promising myself every few seconds that this is the last bite. It doesn’t feel controlled. It feels like my body is moving faster than my brain can stop it.\n\nDoctor: Is making yourself throw up mostly about trying to control your weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly, yes. I mean, I tell myself different things in the moment, like I’m just trying to feel less full or make the panic stop, but underneath it, it’s about weight. It’s about feeling like I ruined everything by eating and I have to get it out of me before it “counts,” if that makes sense.\n\nThat’s actually a big part of why I’m here. I keep making myself throw up after I eat, and it’s gotten to where it doesn’t even feel like a choice sometimes. I’ll eat and then my mind goes straight to, “You have to fix this.” Sometimes I do it in the bathroom at work, which makes me feel so ashamed because I’m trying to be quiet and normal and then go back out like nothing happened. Or I’ll hold it together until I get home and then go straight to the bathroom.\n\nAnd I hate saying this, but it’s started to feel like the only way I can calm down after eating. Like I know it’s not okay, I know it’s disgusting and scary, but in that moment the fear is so loud that throwing up feels like the only thing that quiets it for a little while.\n\nDoctor: How often do you have episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: For months now, it’s been happening most weeks… not every single day, but usually at least a couple times in a week. Sometimes more, depending on how things are going.\n\nIt’s usually like I eat way more than I meant to in a short amount of time, and then afterward I get this awful panic, like I have to undo it somehow. A lot of the time that means making myself throw up, but sometimes I’ll try not eating for a while after, or I’ll exercise because I feel like I have to burn it off. It’s not just once in a while anymore. It’s been this pattern for months, and I keep telling myself it was the last time, but then the next week it happens again.\n\nDoctor: How often do you make yourself throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s often. More often than I want to admit.\n\nA lot of the time after I eat, especially if I feel like I ate too much, my mind goes straight to needing to throw up. Sometimes I’ll do it right away, like if I’m at work I’ll go into the bathroom and try to be quiet and act normal afterward, which makes me feel horrible. Other times I’ll hold it in until I get home, and then it’s like the first thing I do is go to the bathroom because I can’t calm down until I’ve done it.\n\nIt’s not every single time I eat, but it’s become… I don’t know, this regular thing. Like after eating, if that panic starts, throwing up feels like the only way I can make it stop. And I hate that. I hate that it feels like the thing I rely on now, because I know it’s not normal and I feel so ashamed afterward, but in the moment I feel desperate.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your clothes fitting differently due to weight loss?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean… I pay attention to my body a lot, probably too much, but with my clothes it’s hard for me to say if they’re actually fitting differently or if I’m just feeling different in them.\n\nSome days I put something on and I’m convinced it feels looser or tighter, but then I don’t know if that’s real or if I’m just checking and overthinking it. I haven’t had, like, a clear moment where all my clothes are suddenly falling off me or anything like that. I just… I don’t trust my own sense of it very much.\n\nDoctor: Do you make yourself throw up after every episode of eating a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: Not every single time, no… but a lot of the time, yes. Enough that it feels like that’s the pattern now.\n\nIf I have one of those episodes where I eat a lot and I feel like I went too far, it’s like my head immediately starts screaming at me that I have to get it out. Sometimes I try to sit with it for a little while, or I tell myself I’m not going to do it this time, but then I get so worked up and panicky that I end up going to the bathroom anyway.\n\nAnd it’s not just at home, which is part of what scares me. I’ve done it in the bathroom at work, trying to be quiet and then come back out like nothing happened. Other times I wait until I get home, but then it’s almost like I can’t relax or think straight until I’ve thrown up. I hate saying that, because it sounds awful, but it’s started feeling like the only way I can calm down after I eat like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you always feel relieved after making yourself throw up?\n\nPatient: Not always… I mean, at first there’s usually this kind of relief, like the panic drops down a little because I did the thing my mind was telling me I had to do. That’s the part that scares me, honestly, because it’s started feeling like the only way I can calm down after I eat.\n\nBut it’s not a good relief. It’s not like I feel okay afterward. A lot of times I feel ashamed right away, or tired, or like I can’t believe I did it again. If I’m at work and I’ve gone into the bathroom to make myself throw up, then I have to come back out and act normal, like nothing happened, and that feels awful. And if I’ve waited until I get home, sometimes I’m just rushing straight to the bathroom because I’ve been holding all that panic in, and then afterward I’m relieved but also upset with myself.\n\nSo… yes and no. It calms something down in the moment, but then I hate that I needed it to feel calm.\n\nDoctor: How many times a week do you typically eat a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: It’s usually at least a couple times a week… I mean, most weeks. I don’t always write it down or anything, so I’m trying to be honest without giving you some exact number that might not be right, but for months now it’s been happening regularly.\n\nLike, there will be these episodes where I eat way more than I meant to in a short time, and afterward I feel like I have to fix it somehow. A lot of the time that means throwing up, but sometimes I’ll try to make up for it by not eating for a while, or by exercising like I’m trying to burn it off. It kind of depends on where I am and how panicked I feel.\n\nBut it’s not just once in a while anymore. Most weeks, there are at least two times where it happens, sometimes more.\n\nDoctor: How often do you check your weight?\n\nPatient: Too often. I mean… I check it a lot, probably more than I should. Sometimes it’s like I tell myself I’m just going to look once, just to know, but then if the number is even a little higher than what I expected, I panic.\n\nIt can be the smallest change, and my brain treats it like it means everything is ruined. Like I’ve messed up completely and I’m going to just keep gaining and be fat by the end of the day, even though I know that probably sounds dramatic when I say it out loud. But in the moment it feels real.\n\nAnd it’s not only the scale. At work, if my waistband feels tight under my apron, I start focusing on it and I can’t stop. I’ll keep thinking about whether I look bigger, whether other people can tell, whether I’ve let myself go. It makes me feel trapped in my own body. So… I don’t know the exact number, but I check my weight and my body a lot because I’m scared of it going up.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   },

It calms something down in the moment, but then I hate that I needed it to feel calm.\n\nDoctor: How many times a week do you typically eat a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: It’s usually at least a couple times a week… I mean, most weeks. I don’t always write it down or anything, so I’m trying to be honest without giving you some exact number that might not be right, but for months now it’s been happening regularly.\n\nLike, there will be these episodes where I eat way more than I meant to in a short time, and afterward I feel like I have to fix it somehow. A lot of the time that means throwing up, but sometimes I’ll try to make up for it by not eating for a while, or by exercising like I’m trying to burn it off. It kind of depends on where I am and how panicked I feel.\n\nBut it’s not just once in a while anymore. Most weeks, there are at least two times where it happens, sometimes more.\n\nDoctor: How often do you check your weight?\n\nPatient: Too often. I mean… I check it a lot, probably more than I should. Sometimes it’s like I tell myself I’m just going to look once, just to know, but then if the number is even a little higher than what I expected, I panic.\n\nIt can be the smallest change, and my brain treats it like it means everything is ruined. Like I’ve messed up completely and I’m going to just keep gaining and be fat by the end of the day, even though I know that probably sounds dramatic when I say it out loud. But in the moment it feels real.\n\nAnd it’s not only the scale. At work, if my waistband feels tight under my apron, I start focusing on it and I can’t stop. I’ll keep thinking about whether I look bigger, whether other people can tell, whether I’ve let myself go. It makes me feel trapped in my own body. So… I don’t know the exact number, but I check my weight and my body a lot because I’m scared of it going up.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you consider yourself to be underweight?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 36 / laundry_or_dry_cleaning_worker / 9th_12th_no_diploma\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t really know how to answer that… like, I don’t think I’m underweight, no. At least not in the way I picture that word meaning. I don’t look at myself and think, “Oh, I’m too thin.” I’m usually thinking the opposite, honestly, or worrying that I’m bigger than I should be.

But I also don’t know what the “official” line is, if you mean by numbers or a chart or something. I just know I don’t feel underweight. If anything, I feel like I’m always trying to keep from going up. So… no, I wouldn’t consider myself underweight, but I don’t know if I’m seeing myself clearly either.
